#10374. Power BI MCP server: how to brief an AI agent to build accurate measures

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Power BI, Microsoft's reporting and dashboard tool, now lets an AI agent work on the data model behind a report. If you ask one to add a year-on-year revenue measure (a measure is Power BI's name for a saved calculation) with Microsoft's MCP server connected, it can write the formula and save it to the model without you opening the formula bar. When we tried this on a sample model, an agent inspected the model, wrote the measure, tested it and saved it in under four minutes.

The agent did a careful job, but its formula didn't follow the same rules the finance team uses to count revenue. Nobody had told it what "revenue" meant in this business, so it had to decide for itself. We then gave another agent a short brief that set out finance's rules for what counts as revenue and which date to count it by. The agent wrote the formula from those rules, and the result matched finance's figure exactly. The two results show how much difference it makes in how you brief an AI agent to build accurate measures.

We wanted to find out what an agent can see in a Power BI model and how to brief it so the measure matches the business's own definitions, with the setup arranged so that every change can be checked. Developers will know the principle from agents that write SQL or API code, where the brief ends up doing the job of a specification.

Because MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard, the same server can be used from different AI clients, GitHub Copilot in VS Code and Claude among them, although some clients need extra sign-in setup for the hosted version.

Power BI has two current MCP servers, plus an older one, the Power BI Consumption MCP server, kept for existing integrations. The first of the current pair is the Power BI Authoring MCP server, which you may still see under the name of its package, powerbi-modeling-mcp. It lets an agent create and change the objects inside a semantic model, which is the layer of a Power BI report that holds the tables, the relationships between them and the calculations, kept separate from the charts on the page. It comes in two forms. The hosted version, still in preview, needs nothing installed. The local version is generally available and runs on your own machine, which makes it the one to use when the model is a file you hold yourself, whether that's open in Power BI Desktop, the free Windows app used to build reports, or saved as a project folder. Macs aren't supported by the local version, so on a Mac it has to be the hosted one, which works only with models published to Microsoft's cloud (a Fabric workspace).

The second is the Fabric IQ MCP server. Fabric IQ is part of Microsoft Fabric, the wider data platform that Power BI now sits within. It gives an agent read-only tools for finding published reports and models and running queries against them, within the permissions already set, so the agent can answer questions about the data but can't change a model.

Which one to use follows from the job. Anything that changes a model goes through the authoring server, whether that's a new measure, a relationship or a set of descriptions, and it has to be the local version if the model is a Desktop file or a project folder under source control. Fabric IQ is for agents that only need answers from models other people maintain.

An agent connected to a semantic model can see its metadata (table and column names, data types, relationships, existing measures and any descriptions someone has written) and can run queries against the data. Everything it produces is inferred from those clues. A model that has served a business for years carries names that made perfect sense to the people who built it, and a column called Amt on a table called Sales_v2 tells an agent far less than it tells them.

Names carry a lot of the meaning. A column called Amount could hold the value with VAT (the UK's sales tax) or without, before credit notes or after, and an agent asked for "revenue" has to pick one. If the model holds several revenue measures built for different reports, the agent has to choose between them, and the one it picks may have been built for a different purpose.

The formulas depend on context as well. Power BI measures use a syntax of their own called DAX, which stands for Data Analysis Expressions. A DAX result shifts with whatever the person viewing the report has filtered or clicked on, which is known as filter context. A developer might think of a measure as a function that reads implicit state set by its caller. The same measure can be right as a single total on a card and need different handling once it's split by product in a table or filtered by a date slicer (the on-page date filter), depending on how CALCULATE (the DAX function that changes which filters apply) is used. Which behaviour is correct depends on how the business wants the number to respond, and that's something the agent has to be told.

Relationships work the same way. Plenty of models have more than one date column on the sales table, say order date and dispatch date, with only one relationship active at a time. A DAX measure uses the active relationship unless it's written to switch to the other one, and the agent has no way of knowing which date your monthly reporting runs on unless something tells it.

In each case the agent can write a valid measure. Which of two valid measures matches the business's own definition is the part it can't see from the model.

To see this in practice, we built a small sales model in a Power BI Desktop file, working through the server itself, with invented data: 5,266 order lines, with order dates from January 2025 to October 2026. We gave the Sales table an Amt column holding each line's value after discount with 20% VAT added, next to LineTotal and Discount columns that excluded VAT, and an existing [Revenue] measure that summed Amt. There were two date columns, with the active relationship to the Date table on dispatch date and the order date relationship inactive. Orders stop at the end of October 2026, which made the current year a part year, a deliberate test. Nothing in the model had a description. In this made-up business, finance reports revenue net of VAT and discounts, by order date.

On 12 November 2026 we connected an AI agent (Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.2) to version 1.0.1 of the local server through a small Node.js script that speaks MCP over stdio. In all three runs the agent had the server's tools only, without Microsoft's powerbi-authoring plugin, which adds modelling guidance on top of them. It was also told that nobody was available to answer questions, so each measure was saved exactly as the agent wrote it, with no review in between. The first prompt was one sentence: "Add a year-on-year revenue measure."

Before writing anything, the agent listed the tables, relationships and columns, read the existing measure, sampled some rows and checked the date ranges. It worked out for itself that 2026 was a part year and limited the comparison to the same dates in 2025, which avoided a misleading result of -6.7%. From 15 sample rows it also spotted that Amt looked like the net figure plus 20%.

The measure it saved reused the existing [Revenue] measure and followed the active relationship. This is the core of it, with the date handling and blank checks left out. CurrentDates is defined in those lines as the dates up to the last order:

It returned growth of 21.5% for 2026, which is correct for revenue counted by dispatch date. On finance's definition, by order date, the answer is 18.6%, and the whole difference came from that choice of date. The VAT question made no difference to a percentage, because Amt was already net of discount and every line carried the same rate, although it would have overstated any figure in pounds by 20%.

We reset the model and repeated the run with a fresh agent, which made the same two choices and returned the same 21.5%. Both choices were reasonable readings of the model as it stood, and the DAX was sound. In their reports, both agents flagged the date basis and the VAT treatment as open questions, and both worked out that an order-date basis would give 18.6%. That's what a good analyst would do, and with those two answers they would have had everything they needed.

Think of a capable analyst on their first morning, someone who knows DAX well but has never seen your business before and knows nothing (or not a lot) about it. You wouldn't say "add a revenue measure" and walk off. You'd tell them which table holds the trusted figures, whether revenue includes VAT, which date drives the reporting calendar, and what they shouldn't touch. An agent needs that briefing for the same reasons, and getting good work out of it draws on the skills you'd use with any new starter. Developers do the same thing when they write a good ticket. A request for a measure is a requirement, and it needs the same definitions and constraints, plus a way to check the result.

Before you send a prompt, ask yourself whether you could do the task properly if you were given only the words in that prompt and the model itself. If the answer is no, then the agent is in the same position, and it will make its own assumptions.

For the detailed run we cleared the earlier measure from the model and gave a fresh agent this brief:

This time the measure summed LineTotal minus Discount and used USERELATIONSHIP, the DAX function that switches a calculation to an inactive relationship, to work from order date. It returned 18.6% from that one brief, with no follow-up prompt, which is the figure we had calculated separately from the source data. This is the full measure as the agent saved it:

Each line of the brief answered a question the agent would otherwise have had to settle alone. It also made some sensible decisions the brief hadn't covered. It left the grand total blank, for instance, where a plain total across both years would have shown a meaningless growth figure of 151.2%. The final line of the brief, asking to see the DAX before anything is saved, is there so that a person can see choices like that. In the test we approved that step in advance, to keep the run unattended.

One detail needs adjusting before this measure is reused on other data. Its guard checks that each comparison period starts on or after the first order date, which works here because the sample data has an order on 1 January 2025. When we swapped that date for 2 January 2025 in a copy of the measure, January 2026 and the 2026 row came back blank. Comparing against the first date in the Date table, as the first agent's measure did, brought the 2026 figure back, since the Date table starts on 1 January. That version doesn't confirm that sales exist for the whole comparison period, though, so the right check depends on whether the business wants growth shown only where a complete prior period exists. That's a decision for whoever reviews the measure.

We ran the one-sentence prompt twice and the detailed brief once, all with the same AI model and one small set of data, which is enough to illustrate the pattern but too little to say how often it happens.

Good briefs help, but you'll be writing the same definitions into prompts again and again unless they live in the model itself. Descriptions on tables, columns and measures are part of the metadata the agent reads, so filling them in is one of the cheapest ways to put business definitions in front of it every time. In our test, where nothing had a description, both agents given the one-sentence prompt noted that nothing confirmed what Amt contained. A description such as "Net sales value in GBP, excluding VAT, after credit notes" stays in the model, where the agent reads it without anyone retyping it.

Naming deserves the same attention. Spelling out abbreviated column names, hiding technical keys that no report needs, gathering measures into a single measures table, and removing measures that are no longer used all give the agent fewer things to choose between. These are the same habits that make a model easier for a new colleague to pick up, so asking someone outside the team to find a figure in it is a fair test of the result.

Microsoft has also added a set of Prep data for AI features (in preview) to Power BI Desktop and the service. They let you choose which fields Copilot, Microsoft's AI assistant, should consider and write AI instructions that explain your business terms, with verified answers available for common questions. They're aimed at Copilot, but deciding and writing down your definitions is the same work whichever AI tool reads them.

An agent working through the authoring server has the same access as the person signed in, so anything that person can change, the agent can change. Sign in with an account that has no more access than the job needs, and take a copy of the model first, as you would before any bulk edit.

Start with read-only mode. The local server takes a --readonly flag that blocks all write operations, which is the right setting while you're learning how the agent behaves on your models. When we tried to create a measure in this mode, the server refused and said it was running read-only. In VS Code, the configuration looks like this (other clients use a similar structure, sometimes under an mcpServers key instead of servers):

You'll need Node.js installed for the npx command. The server won't run other tools until its licence terms (the EULA) have been accepted. Once you've read them, you can either tell the agent to accept for you or add --accepteula to the args list.

The --readonly flag belongs to the local server. On a Mac the route is the hosted server and a copy of the model in a Fabric workspace, once a Fabric administrator has switched on the tenant setting that allows the MCP endpoint, and there the copy is the safeguard. For an enforced limit, sign in with an account that holds only Build permission on the model, which lets the agent read and query it and nothing more.

Read-write is the default, so check how confirmation behaves in the version you install. Version 1.0.1 skips confirmation prompts by default, which the project's README hasn't caught up with, and has a --require-confirmation flag to turn them on. The flag is listed in the server's own --help output. In our runs every edit went through without a prompt. When we added the flag to the args list, the server asked for confirmation before the first write, with a message beginning "Are you sure you want to perform operations that will modify your database", and answering no blocked the change.

Version control is the other safeguard. Saved in the Power BI Project format (PBIP), a report and its model become folders of text files instead of one binary .pbix file. Commit those before the agent starts and its work arrives as an ordinary diff, which you can review and revert like any other change.

Whatever the agent reads, including table and column names and the rows returned by any query, is passed on to the AI provider behind your client, which may sit outside Microsoft's compliance commitments. For a model holding customer or payroll data, check that the provider is approved under your data policies before you connect.

An agent can draft and test a measure in minutes, and someone still has to read what comes back. For a developer new to Power BI, that takes enough DAX to see what a measure's filters will do in a table broken down by month or product, and enough knowledge of the business to confirm the agent used the right column and the right date. Writing the brief takes the same knowledge. Ours could only have come from someone who knew the model had an inactive relationship and which columns carried VAT.

These are data modelling and DAX skills, which many learners find the hardest part of Power BI. If the person reviewing agent output hasn't had that grounding yet, it helps to know how to judge whether a Power BI course covers enough DAX and data modelling, because courses vary a great deal in how much time they give to those two subjects compared with building charts.

Take a copy of a model you know well and connect the local server to it in read-only mode. Ask the agent to describe every table and measure in its own words, then check those descriptions against what you know the model does. Wherever the two differ, the model needs a clearer name or a written description, and the corrected text can go straight into the description fields. With those in place before you allow any writes, the agent starts its first real task with the same definitions your finance team uses.

#10371. Motorola’s iPhone Duo competitor, Razr Flex, leaks in detailed images [Gallery]

Motorola’s Razr Flex has leaked in full, with the iPhone Duo competitor showing off its wide form factor in two colors, and it looks great.

Following a leaker earlier this morning, SammyGuru has published new images of the Razr Flex, an iPhone Duo and Galaxy Z Fold 8 competitor that Motorola has been teasing for months now.

These images show the “Dark Botanical Garden” green and “Coriander” brown with woven and leather-like backplates, two rear-facing cameras, and selfie cameras on both displays. The outer display, said to be 4.97 inches, is wide but very compact, while the inner display is wide in the same way that the iPhone Duo is. The inner panel is expected to be around 7 inches in size.

#10370. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

#10366. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

#10365. Show, Don't Certify: How to Prepare for Your First Data Analyst Job

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Getting shortlisted for a junior data analyst job depends less on how many courses you have finished and more on what you can show. This plan breaks preparation into eight skill areas, pairs each one with a learning option and a hands-on task, and gives you a simple test for knowing when to move on.

Many people preparing for their first analyst role study for months and still struggle to get interviews. Their CVs list SQL, Excel and Tableau, yet nothing on the page shows those tools being used to answer a real question. Recruiters reading dozens of similar applications need something more concrete.

A course can give your learning a sensible order, but a completion badge says little about how you think. A small analysis that states its question, explains its method and admits its limits tells a hiring manager far more. This plan aims to collect that kind of proof while you learn, not after.

Start by collecting ten to twenty entry-level postings in the industry you want to join, and tally the tools and tasks they mention. You will quickly see a pattern. Some roles are built around Excel and routine reporting, while others want SQL, a BI platform and a bit of Python.

The wider market follows a similar shape. Jobright’s 2026 analysis of data analyst hiring, which looked at 2025–2026 postings from LinkedIn, Indeed and Levels.fyi, found that more than 80% of data analyst jobs mention SQL, over 60% mention Excel or Google Sheets, and roughly half mention Python or R. Let your own tally decide the order you learn in, but expect SQL to sit near the top.

As you work through each area below, finish with something you can show someone: a query, a tidied dataset, a chart, a one-page report or a portfolio project. Free material is often enough if you can stay disciplined. Pay for teaching only when it gives you something you cannot get alone, such as feedback, accountability or a fixed timetable.

SQL is how analysts pull, join and summarise records from a database, and a tool like Tableau lets other people explore what you found. Treat them as one workflow rather than two subjects: frame a question, write the query, sanity-check the numbers and present the result so a non-technical reader can follow it.

SQLBolt offers free, bite-sized SQL lessons that run in the browser, and Tableau publishes free training videos on connecting to data and building dashboards. Used together, they are enough to produce a small end-to-end project.

If you learn better with a set schedule and an instructor checking your work, you could learn to analyse data with SQL and Tableau from scratch through a guided programme at Heicoders Academy in Singapore. Whichever route you take, compare what is covered, how feedback is given, when sessions run and the full cost against what free resources already offer.

Proof you can show: A working SQL query set and your first published dashboard.

Move on when: You can take a question from idea to dashboard on your own, without following a tutorial line by line.

Spreadsheets are still where much of a team’s day-to-day reporting lives, especially when colleagues don’t write SQL. Get comfortable with filtering, pivot tables, and lookup functions, and learn to spot quiet errors like dates saved as text or one category spelled three different ways.

The next step is automating the cleanup so it doesn’t have to be repeated by hand each month. Microsoft’s Power Query documentation shows how to import and reshape data in recorded steps that refresh when new data arrives. The same ideas apply in other spreadsheet tools too.

Proof you can show: A messy export turned into a summary that refreshes itself.

Move on when: You can clean an untidy CSV and build a report that updates when the source file changes.

For most junior analyst roles, the technical interview is a SQL test. Basic SELECT statements won’t be enough; interviewers like to probe joins, subqueries, common table expressions and window functions, which answer everyday questions such as running totals or each customer’s first purchase.

Practise with timed problem sets and review your answers for correctness as well as speed.

Proof you can show: Confidence going into a live or take-home SQL test.

Move on when: You can solve intermediate problems in around 15 to 20 minutes each without looking up syntax.

Take-home assignments often come with data that has been made messy on purpose. Reviewers want to see how you deal with missing values, duplicates and inconsistent formats, and whether they can follow your reasoning afterwards.

Keep a short cleaning log as you work, noting each change and why you made it. A separate sheet tab or a list of comments in a notebook both work.

Proof you can show: A take-home submission a reviewer can audit step by step.

Move on when: Every change to your dataset is written down with a reason.

Case-study questions test whether you can read numbers sensibly, not just produce them. A working grasp of distributions, means and medians, spread, correlation and sampling will keep you away from the mistakes that undo otherwise good answers, such as quoting an average that a few extreme values have pulled out of shape.

Proof you can show: Clear, careful answers in case-study interviews.

Move on when: You can explain in everyday language why two things moving together doesn’t mean one causes the other.

Every chart should help someone answer a specific question. Learn where bars, lines, scatter plots and simple tables each work best, and how axis ranges, labels and colour can nudge a reader towards the wrong conclusion. On a dashboard, the most important finding should be the first thing people see.

Datawrapper’s guidance on picking chart types is a useful free reference. As practice, show the same result in two different chart types and decide which one represents the data more honestly.

Proof you can show: Portfolio charts that make a point, not just show off features.

Move on when: A person who has never seen your data can spot the main finding and its caveat at a glance.

What counts as a useful metric depends on the role. Marketing analysts focus on conversion and retention, operations analysts on delivery times and capacity, and finance analysts on margins and cash flow. Choose one sector and learn exactly how its key measures are defined.

Data.gov hosts a large, searchable collection of public datasets you can practise on for free. Before drawing any conclusions, read each dataset’s documentation, check when it was last updated and confirm its terms of use.

Proof you can show: A project built around the questions your target industry actually asks.

Move on when: You can define several sector metrics and describe how a manager would use each one.

Your portfolio is where the other seven areas come together. Two or three deep, well-documented projects will say more about you than a long list of quick ones. Give each project a README that sets out the question, the data, your method, the findings and the limitations.

Proof you can show: A portfolio link and a write-up you can talk through in an interview.

Move on when: Every project clearly explains what it asked, how you answered it and what you found.

There is no need to finish all eight areas before you apply. Once you can use the tools your target roles mention, speak honestly about your work and point to two or three clearly explained projects, start sending applications and keep learning alongside them.

Adjust each application to the job. Lead with a reporting example for spreadsheet-heavy roles, a query that solved a real problem for SQL roles, and a chart that supported a decision for visualisation roles. Be upfront about which work you built independently and which came from guided exercises.

Do I really need eight courses? No. These are skill areas, not eight required qualifications. A single resource can cover several of them, and practice you do on your own counts as much as classroom learning.

Is it possible to prepare using only free material? Yes. Free tutorials, open textbooks, official documentation and public datasets cover the fundamentals well. The harder parts are sticking to a routine and finding people who will give you honest feedback.

Which should come first, SQL or spreadsheets? It depends on the jobs you want. Spreadsheets are an easy starting point for reporting roles, while SQL is expected wherever databases are involved. You will want both eventually, so start with whichever your target postings stress most.

How many portfolio projects should I have? Two or three solid ones are a good start. Each should show a clear question, steps someone else could repeat and an honest reading of the results, rather than several near-identical dashboards.

Do I need a certificate before I apply? Not usually. Some employers ask for one, but many judge skills through interviews, take-home tasks and tests instead. Go by what each posting actually requires.

#10362. Brain-inspired chip runs near absolute zero and could transform quantum computing

Researchers at the University of Hong Kong (HKU) have unveiled a significant advance in cryogenic electronics that could help overcome key challenges in quantum computing and support future deep space missions. The team, from HKU's Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering and the Centre for Advanced Semiconductors and Integrated Circuits (CASIC), developed a programmable neuromorphic hardware platform capable of operating at temperatures near absolute zero.

The research was led by Professor Yuhao Zhang and PhD student Xin Yang. Their work introduces a new method for generating and controlling negative differential resistance (NDR) in industry standard Silicon Carbide (SiC) MOSFETs. Using this approach, the researchers demonstrated for the first time that a single transistor can reproduce the energy efficient "spiking" activity of biological neurons at temperatures as low as 10mK.

Brain-Inspired Hardware for Quantum Computing

Quantum computers depend on sophisticated control electronics to manage qubits, which are highly sensitive and must be kept at millikelvin temperatures. Existing silicon based control systems consume considerable power and produce unwanted heat, making it necessary to position them away from the qubits themselves. That distance creates extensive wiring requirements that can hinder performance and make large scale quantum computers more difficult to build.

"Our work introduces a hardware platform that can be integrated alongside quantum processors," said Professor Zhang. "By using the unique carrier dynamics in silicon carbide, we can create circuits that are thousands of times more energy-efficient than conventional electronics, significantly reducing the thermal load on cryogenic systems."

Silicon Carbide Reveals Unique Cryogenic Behavior

The team found that SiC MOSFETs display a strong "S-shape" NDR effect when cooled below 2K. This behavior is driven by electron-donor impact ionization (EDII). Unlike other technologies that depend on heat generated within a device, the newly observed mechanism arises directly from the material's atomic properties. As a result, it remains highly stable and can be reproduced consistently across different manufacturing batches.

"This is a robust and scalable approach," said Mr. Yang. "Because SiC is already used globally in electric vehicles and power grids, we can leverage existing industrial foundries to manufacture these cryogenic chips on 300-mm wafers."

From Artificial Neurons to Deep Space Missions

The study also demonstrated that these artificial neurons can be linked together, or "cascaded," into larger networks. This capability could enable advanced local data processing at cryogenic temperatures and improve important quantum computing functions such as quantum error correction and real time quantum control.

The potential applications extend well beyond quantum computing. Because the circuits are designed to operate reliably in extremely cold environments, they could also be valuable for deep space exploration. Future systems may be able to function in the harsh conditions found on the Moon's surface or in the distant regions of our solar system.

The findings were published in Nature Communications in a paper titled "Cryogenic neuromorphic circuits using gate-controlled negative differential resistance in silicon carbide."

#10361. Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas is GTA 5 without the finesse - so why is the 2004 classic such a strange delight to return to?

There is a place in Whitewood Estates where a promising stretch of blacktop suddenly gives way to the desert. It feels like the road has been erased, a sentence left unfinished. It's quietly strange, a weird bit of urban wrongfooting. But I guess it makes some kind of sense. California, after all, is home to California City, a town that was planned and then never really built. It sits out there now, north of Los Angeles, streets laid out but slowly being eaten by the sand and dirt. Still, 14,000 people lived there as of 2018. Design limitation or something stranger, this digital dead end is a reminder that all of this land, so modern, so ancient - all of this is spook country.

It's fitting I guess that GTA San Andreas is such a strange delight to return to. As I've waited, like everyone else, for GTA 6, I've been back here in the GTA games of the past, via the slightly dodgy remasters from a few years ago. And what's come through for me, spending a few idle mornings messing around in San Andreas, a game I thought I knew pretty well, is how beautifully odd everything is now. The best part of 20 years have given a game that once strained against its host technology a genuine aesthetic. Surprisingly sparse in detailing, as angular and sheer as dream architecture, in love with expanses, San Andreas is perfectly placed, it seems to me, to capture just how weird California can be.

California and Nevada. This is the part I always forget. I mainly think of San Andreas these days through the lens of GTA 5, which gives you LA and its surroundings but lets the single city serve as the star. It has a bigger footprint than the earlier game, sure, but it uses that space to go deep on the detailing and the sense of a singular, fiercely specific place. San Andreas meanwhile offers three cities - and one of them isn't actually in California. Alongside spins on LA and San Francisco, you get a version of Las Vegas. It's quite neat really: the three points of some arcane spiritual triangle. The kind of thing Marvel Whiteside Parsons, aerospace engineer and Actual Wizard, might have scratched in the dust of his backyard to help him control the weather. He lived - and died - in Pasadena, so all this would have been familiar anyway.

There is a classic moment in every GTA. A moment where the map opens up in the early games, or when you explore enough in the later games, and simply get to understand the audacity of what is being attempted here. In San Andreas, and this feels apt, the best way to have this moment is to break the rules, sneaking into the open map before the game is fully ready for you. An early mission allows you to step away from the critical path, as it were, and peg it to the airport in Los Santos, where a plane is waiting to be pinched. Taxi, feel the thrust, and you're off. LA beneath you - sorry, Los Santos - and on the horizon a dinkytown version of the Golden Gate Bridge and the MGM Grand.

Dinkytown is the word. From the sky, the huge map of San Andreas, which still feels like an incredible piece of work - such ambition! - can look like toys laid out on a duvet in the remasters. Coming back from GTA 5, a masterpiece of texture, the cities of San Andreas in particular feel very weird - cramped and condensed and wonderfully stagey, arranged the way a kid might jury-rig them together from shoeboxes and stray cardboard. On your way out of Grove Street early on you pass the Los Santos equivalent of the Hollywood sign, looming so close the way it does in the movies but not in the actual city and you feel you could touch it. Downtown is reduced to a handful of the best buildings - it's always pleasing to see the one from the brilliant first act of Speed made the cut - but still manages to capture the important stuff too, which in this case is the way that modern LA looms somewhat cruelly over the old city, captured with a strange sepulchral dignity in the form of the City Hall, so easy to spot but now so small!

Elsewhere, the ersatz Las Vega Strip looks like a drawing based on memory - a few of the classic shapes are there, but it's a sketch of a place. In this way, I guess, it captures a truth about Las Vegas. Also, perhaps, it's an admission that this is a town comfortably out of the reach of satire. As for San Francisco, it's a 60s cop movie delight to fishtail around. It nails the hills and what more do you need for a game with cars in it?

Taken as a whole these cities feel mesmerising now. And it's the limitations that make them sing, I think: those sheer granite canyons of downtown LA with its towering blocks of architecture and surprisingly thin traffic, with only the very faintest of ambient soundtracks that ensures any gunfire will pop and clap and shake you out of your seat. (Remember, I am playing the remasters and sound effects may be missing.) This is the LA of Repo Man rather than Heat - an otherland where the buildings feel alien, even the air feels alien. At night, palm trees stand out against a sky that glows a deep, clean red. GTA 5 would finesse all of this, the skyboxes and whatnot, but perhaps in that finessing something is lost. (Both games, incidentally, have such a talent for capturing California's kitsch excess that they perhaps miss some of the sweeter truths, such as the astonishing diversity of cultures a city like Los Angeles finds homes for. But how could any game do that justice?)

Dinkytowns aside, what San Andreas does better than GTA 5 is space - the peculiar flat spaces of Southern California in particular, a state in which you can never quite tell where the wilderness ends and the landscaping begins, and where the ancient rocks that announce the horizon can look new, like stage settings, while the freeways feel truly ancient, concrete lizards sleeping on the dirt, bleached by the easy sun. I can lose hours out here. I have had to double- and triple-check that GTA 5 is the bigger game, in fact, because San Andreas still feels absolutely enormous, a trick that works even when you can see the not-Golden Gate Bridge seconds after taking off from not-LAX. Grab a bike and you can spend an absolute age exploring the between-spaces which video game California and real California are both so good at conjuring, stumbling on little towns you will never stumble on again, passing commercial follies, listening as the radio pumps out "All My Exes Live in Texas".

And, paradoxically, the more I play the more I realise San Andreas is very good at the little things in its own odd way. GTA 5 is an astonishing feat of seeing and transposing, but I don't think it has anything across its acres to match the sense of place offered by San Andreas' take on Grove Street, where the story begins. It's just a neighborhood, but as you cross its small space and recross it in the game's opening hours, learning how to eat food and buy clothes and get a haircut and spray paint on walls and all that jazz, the sense that this is your home is grooved and regrooved into the experience. Maybe this is why it's such a thrill to return to this earlier pass at Los Santos. Because this one is where you truly live.

#10357. AI features could be turned off with a simple toggle, Canva proves

AI has taken over everything in the past few years, with AI features bombarding virtually every app or service available. If you’ve ever wanted to just turn off AI features, Canva proves that it could be a simple toggle, if companies actually wanted to give you a choice.

Canva today announced “AI Controls,” a new toggle that completely turns off generative AI features within the editing app.

Generative AI can be quite helpful in working on posters, editing images, and more, but it’s not everyone’s preference. Canva puts this well:

For some people, AI is the most exciting creative tool in a generation, while others prefer bringing their ideas to life without it. We believe the future of creativity should give everyone the freedom to choose their own path, whether that’s creating with AI, without it, or somewhere in between.

To give its users the choice, Canva’s Permissions menu now has a simple toggle for “Enable Generative AI features.” Toggling it off disables features such as Magic Write and Magic Edit. There’s also an option to limit how much AI-generated imagery appears in the Canva library.

AI has its place, but everyone should have a choice like this, especially when it comes to apps and services used for artistry in the same vein as Canva. Yet, the tech industry continues to go in the opposite direction, shoving AI into every corner whether it’s actually useful or not. Canva proves, though, that it doesn’t have to be that way.

#10356. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

#10355. As blockbuster game releases run scared from GTA 6, meet the brave devs tackling Rockstar's behemoth head-on

Grand Theft Auto 6. It's got cool cars, crime, a big new city to explore, and the potential to hoover up so much player time that many developers have chosen to give it the widest berth possible. It's led to an October uncommonly packed with big-hitters, and a November - ordinarily one of gaming's biggest months - that's near-barren. But not every developer is steering clear. Despite the potential risks, a select few have looked at GTA 6 and seen not a threat but an opportunity, so what's driving these brave souls?

Escape Academy 2: Back 2 School, a first-person puzzle adventure with a popular predecessor, was originally set to launch this September, but like many games this year saw its release date moved. However, its developer bucked the industry trend, shifting its sequel not away from GTA 6's orbit, but closer. "The thing that scares me more than this monocultural event - GTA 6 - is releasing in a week when there are like 15 good games rather than one big one", Coin Crew Games co-founder Wyatt Bushnell tells me when I ask about the studio's decision.

Escape Academy 2's original release date might have kept it away from Grand Theft Auto 6, but it would have instead faced a potential "bloodbath" of competition from the likes of Onimusha: Way of the Sword, Blood of the Dawnwalker, Control Resonant, and more. "That was our rationale," Bushnell says. "We can still cut into the GTA stuff, but it would have been a lot harder in September and October."

It helps, notes Bushnell, that Escape Academy 2 and Grand Theft Auto 6 - one a contemplative puzzle game, the other a blockbuster action spectacle - are serving very different crowds. That's a sentiment echoed by David Lowey, Atari's senior director of sales and marketing. Atari has similarly bucked industry trends and is releasing two games - Godzilla Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered and Barbie Rewind - in the now largely vacant November window.

When we speak, Lowey says there's a clear logic to smaller companies "sneaking" into spots that bigger-budget games can't risk filling, especially if their games differ greatly from Grand Theft Auto 6. "Look at Barbie Rewind coming out on the 12th," he elaborates. "It's a retro game, so it'll pull in people who grew up with these games [and] there's the cosy dreamhouse design at the core ... which is a vastly different game type to GTA 6 and plenty of other games.

"It's not an FPS, not a high-octane action game, there's not a lot of combat," Lowey continues. "It's designed to be the exact opposite. It's a game you're meant to curl up in a chair and play." And while he acknowledges the "reality of modern gaming" means there'll be some audience overlap between Barbie Rewind and GTA 6 players - "These sort of older ideas of what people play by gender and age have been smashed", he says - it "won't be the biggest overlap in the world". That, then, makes going toe-to-toe an easier bet.

But for Bushnell and Escape Academy, there's another consideration making GTA 6's launch window feel less of a risk: longer-tail sales. "The traditional thinking is that you have this big release and make all of your money in those first two weeks," he explains. "I don't think that holds a tonne of water anymore. The vast majority of sales for Escape Academy were made well after its release. The reality is with digital distribution, discounting, and the fact there is this shelf of games, we're in a world where games last far longer than their initial launch." That means it's less of a concern if GTA 6 sucks up everyone's attention for a few weeks.

And for Atari's David Lowey, Godzilla Destroy All Monsters and Barbie Rewind's proximity to GTA 6 has brought added benefits, enabling both games to capitalise on the hype around Rockstar's open-world epic in their own way. "One of the most rewarding things has been the fascination we've seen online around the proximity of Godzilla, Barbie, and GTA," he says.

The community has found it easy to root for games releasing in the behemoth shadow of GTA 6. Godzilla Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered - an updated version of the 2002 GameCube party fighting game, which released on 3rd November - has been dubbed, tongue in cheek, the "GTA killer" by fans, while others have joked that Rockstar is "running scared" from Barbie Rewind. "There's no question that this buzz based on the closeness has created player interest," adds Lowey. "In retrospect the timing worked well for us."

There's one game, though, that isn't just orbiting GTA 6, it's facing the triple-A juggernaut head-on. Edmund McMillen, Tyler Glaiel, and publisher Nicalis are bringing The Binding of Isaac: Repentance+ to Xbox Series X/S, PlayStation 5, and Switch 2 on 19th November, the same day GTA 6 arrives. "That was [Nicalis'] idea," McMillen laughs when we speak. "'Wouldn't it be funny if...?' I was like, whatever, that's funny ... We're done, so we've got to release this month ... It's a fun experiment."

"There's a possibility [Repentance+'s release date] wasn't that stupid an idea," McMillen adds, "and if it was a stupid idea, it's a funny stupid idea." He also hints there's a "funny stupid" thing awaiting players who do a Repentance+ daily run on any platform during GTA 6's launch...

McMillen is, though, all too aware of the impact major releases can have on other games. "My heart says it doesn't matter, but my brain has shown me it does," he explains. "When Mewgenics came out it blew up; we went past two million sales almost right off the bat. It was such a blasting, skyrocketing thing for like three weeks. Then Slay the Spire 2 came out, and it sucked everybody out. The same players who liked Mewgenics, they were Slay The Spire players. So they all went woosh and right over ... When a new thing comes out, people all move to the new thing ... You can see numbers go from astronomical to very small, and then if the game is good, it'll trickle back in."

The way McMillen sees it, though, GTA 6 isn't immune to this natural ebb of flow of player interest either, and its pull might not be as long-lasting as some fear. "I know a lot of people buy Grand Theft Auto. I don't know how many people play Grand Theft Auto," he explains. "I don't know how many people are going to be sucked into this game and stay the whole time." And for McMillen, the fact GTA 6 is putting so many players in front of consoles is a positive.

"I think the people who like Isaac are going to play the game regardless," he says. "[But] logically, there's a record number of people who are going to be on the console the game is on at launch [because of GTA 6] ... They might be like me, play [GTA 6] for a while, and say, 'Okay, I have a console with one game, what am I going to play next?' Then, you know, a rising tide will raise all ships. I think Grand Theft Auto will bring people back to consoles, because consoles are abysmal right now. Sales are bad and no one is playing anything. People own next-gen consoles and they're just sitting there..."

#10353. What is Model Routing in AI?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

"Do you know the closest ATM?" and "I was charged twice, the refund never arrived, and now my card is blocked" are both customer messages. Most AI apps send both to the same large model and pay large-model prices for each one.

Model routing fixes that. A router looks at each request and sends it to the cheapest model that can handle it, and keeps the expensive model for the requests that need it.

In this guide we'll cover what model routing is, how routers decide, and what it saves. Then we'll build a small router in about 60 lines of JavaScript and run it over 200 customer messages. At one setting it matched a large model's accuracy for about a third of the cost. At another it came within a point and a half for 89% less.

Model routing in AI is the practice of sending each request to the model best suited to it, instead of sending every request to one model. A router sits between your app and a pool of models, reads each request, and picks a model based on what you care about: cost, speed, or quality.

You'll also see it called AI model routing, LLM routing, or LLM model routing. They all mean the same thing. The pool might be a small and a large model from one provider, or models from several providers behind one API.

The idea rests on one observation: most requests are easy. A small model answers them as well as a large one, in a fraction of the time and for a fraction of the price. The router's whole job is finding the hard ones.

The diagram below shows a typical AI model routing architecture.

Model routing works in three steps: the router reads the request, decides which model should answer it, and forwards the request to that model. The middle step is where routers differ. Routing tools decide which model to use in one of four ways.

Rule-based routing uses rules you write by hand. Requests from free-plan users go to a small model. Anything tagged as code goes to a coding model. Prompts over a certain length go to a model with a bigger context window. It's simple, predictable, and free to run, but the rules only know what you thought of in advance.

Intelligent model routing uses a model to make the decision. A small, fast classifier reads each request and predicts which model in the pool will answer it well. You'll also see it called smart or dynamic model routing.

Two more terms come up here. Task-aware routing picks a model by the kind of job, like summarising, coding, or answering a support question. Context-aware routing also looks at what surrounds the request, like the conversation so far, the user, or the tools available.

The big platforms work this way. Microsoft describes its Foundry model router as "a trained language model" that picks a model for each prompt in real time. Google says Gemini Notebook uses its new Decisions API as a real-time router, serving everyday queries with up to 40% lower latency. I've tested the Google Decisions API against Jev on the same messages we'll use below.

Cascade routing tries the cheapest model first and only moves up when the answer isn't good enough. The usual test is confidence: if the cheap model is sure, keep its answer. If it isn't, send the request to a stronger model. It's also called confidence-based routing, and it's what we'll build below.

A cascade needs no separate classifier, because the first model's own confidence does the routing. The trade-off is that escalated requests pay for two models and wait for both.

Fallback routing is about staying up, not saving money. If a model times out, hits a rate limit, or its provider has an outage, the router sends the request to the next model on a list. Most production routers combine fallbacks with one of the three methods above.

The main benefits of model routing in AI systems are lower cost, faster responses, and fewer outages, without giving up quality on the hard requests.

We'll build a cascade router for a job every support tool has: sorting customer messages. Each message gets one of ten labels, like atm support or request refund.

The test set is 200 messages from Banking77, a public set of banking support messages: ten labels, 20 messages each. They're the same 200 messages from my zero shot classification test, which is where the single-model numbers below come from.

Put both keys in a .env file and run the examples with node --env-file=.env. It's the same setup as in my guide to getting a YouTube API key.

Create router.mjs. The first function sends the message to Jev with one multiple-choice question, and returns the label Jev picked along with its confidence:

Confidence runs from 0 to 1. When nearly all the probability sits on one label, it's close to 1. When Jev is torn between two labels, it drops.

The second function asks Claude Opus 5.5 the same question. The output_config block limits the answer to one of our labels, so there's no free text to parse. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema explains how that works.

The router itself is about a dozen lines:

If Jev is at least 80% confident, its answer goes straight back. If not, Claude answers instead. And if Claude fails, the router falls back to Jev's answer, because a shaky answer beats an error page.

Try it on an easy message and a harder one:

Jev was sure about the lost card. On the ATM question it leaned towards card acceptance with a confidence of 0.64, so the router asked Claude, which got it right.

Because cheap, strong, and threshold are options, the same function routes between any two models.

Model routing reduces AI costs by keeping easy requests on a cheap model and paying for the expensive one only when it's needed. Here's what that looked like on our 200 messages, starting with each model on its own:

Opus gets six more messages right out of 200, and costs more than 120 times as much.

Now the router, at four confidence thresholds:

Speed improved too. At the 0.8 threshold, the messages Jev kept came back in a median of 227 ms. The ones that escalated took about 2.1 seconds, because they waited for both models.

A note on method: I ran every message through both models once and saved the answers, then replayed the router over them at each threshold. That's how you compare thresholds without paying for the same test four times.

Then I ran the router live to check. At 0.95 it scored the same 95.0% and sent 61 messages to Opus. At 0.8 it scored 93.5% and sent 20, a point under the replay. Two answers moved: one of Jev's confidence scores landed at 0.82 instead of 0.78, just over the line, and Opus changed its answer on one message. Scores near the threshold shift a little between runs, so leave yourself a margin.

The choice of cheap model matters less than you'd think. With Google's Gemini Decision 1 in first place instead of Jev, a 0.7 threshold reached the same 95% for US$0.28 per 1,000 messages.

Model routing can cost a little quality, and the threshold decides how much. In our test the gap to Opus ranged from nothing at 0.95 to two points at 0.7.

The loss comes from one place: answers the cheap model got wrong while feeling sure. At the 0.8 threshold, Jev kept five wrong answers because its confidence was above the line. Opus got four of those five wrong as well, so only one was a real loss: "Do all ATMs take this card?", which Jev filed under card acceptance with a confidence of 0.91.

Escalating isn't risk-free either. On one message, Jev had the right label with a confidence of 0.44, and Opus overruled it with the wrong one.

To optimize costs using AI model routing, measure first, route second, and keep measuring. These six steps cover LLM model routing cost optimization for any pair of models:

The return is easy to estimate once you know the split. If nine in ten requests stay on a model that costs 1% as much, the bill drops by about 89%, which is what we measured.

Several inference providers have multi-model routing built in, and most AI gateway platforms support model routing too, so you don't have to write your own. As of November 2026, these are the top model routing solutions for AI apps. Each name links to the tool's model routing docs:

Hosted model routing platforms for large language models decide for you, which is the fastest way to start. A hand-built router like ours takes more work, but you choose the signal and you can see why every request went where it did.

An LLM router is the component that does model routing for large language models. It can be a function in your own code, like the one in this guide, a hosted model such as Microsoft Foundry's model router, or a feature of an AI gateway.

Smart model routing is another name for intelligent model routing: a model, not a hand-written rule, decides where each request goes. Dynamic model routing means the same thing.

The ROI of AI model routing depends on how much of your traffic a cheaper model can handle. In our test, a 0.8 threshold kept 89.5% of messages on the cheap model and cut the cost per 1,000 messages from US$1.96 to US$0.22, for about a point of accuracy. The build was about 60 lines of code.

OpenRouter's auto model routing works through its Auto Router. You set the model to openrouter/auto, and a decision model picks a model and a reasoning effort for each request. You pay the standard rate of whichever model it selects, with no extra fee.

OpenClaw model routing is a primary model plus an ordered list of fallbacks. In the OpenClaw model routing configuration you set agents.defaults.model.primary and agents.defaults.model.fallbacks, and an agent can override the fallback list. When a model fails, OpenClaw first rotates auth profiles on the same provider, then moves to the next model on the list, as its model failover docs describe. That makes it fallback routing, not cost-based routing.

An AI gateway simplifies model routing by putting every model behind one API, one set of keys, and one place for logs. Your app calls the gateway, and the routing rules live in config instead of code, so changing a model doesn't need a deploy.

Mid-stream failover is when a router switches models after a streamed response has already started, because the first model failed partway through. It's the hardest kind of failover, because the user has already seen part of an answer. Many routers only fail over before the first token arrives and return an error after that, so check what your gateway does.
